| 2026-09-25 | (implements D169; how calls are followed proposed by Claude, unconfirmed) **A call undoes a proof only if it may change what the proof reads.** Before generating, the compiler studies every function of the program once (`CallEffects`): which attributes it assigns (by class and name), which lists it shrinks (an attribute's, a parameter's, or unknown), and what it calls, resolving a receiver's class from declared types and falling back to every function of that name; the effects are closed over the call graph. | 2026-09-25 | (implements D178, D150 and D151; the readings below proposed by Claude, unconfirmed) **`Memory` is a namespace: `Memory.Address` is the place, `Memory.Heap` the default allocator, and the singleton `Memory` with its free functions is gone.** `Memory.Address` (`library/memory/address.spite`) is a number class of eight bytes that casts to and from `Long` like every number, so `address + 16` is an address and a foreign function's `Long` result is one by assignment. Every `var memory = Memory()` in `library/` is `var heap = Memory.Heap()`, which also leaves the name `memory` to D152's `.memory`. `TypedMemory` takes a `Memory.Address`, and its `read_value`, `write_value` and `release_value` lend an address the way a read does, so a frame-placed allocation may go through them (D108's placement rule, widened). Not built: D178's allocation from operating-system pages and copying and comparing through `DynamicLibrary` -- the heap is still `malloc` and `copy_to` is `memmove`. Self-compile 1.7 s before and after (the reads were one C call each and are one C statement now). `conformance/stage6/memory_floor` (now over `TypedMemory`), `placed_free`, `diagnostics/memory_access`, `docs/memory.md`, `docs/optimizations.md`; questions in `mortaros_missing_decisions.md` 142-143. |

The same works after `List<T>()`, `Dictionary<T>()` and `.copy()` of a class whose copy the compiler writes. Anywhere else -- a later line, an attribute, a number or `String` -- is an error: "'spark' was already used, so its allocator can no longer change ... 'var moved = spark.copy()' and then 'moved.memory.allocator = ...'" (D153), or "only an object gets an allocator", or "'Console' is not an allocator" (`diagnostics/allocator_after_use`). An allocator is any class answering `allocate(bytes: Long): Memory.Address` and `free(address: Memory.Address)`, named on that line (a name or a path of names, so evaluating it before the constructor changes nothing); `Memory.Heap` is the default and a no-op. `Memory.Arena(block_bytes)` is Spite over the heap: 16-byte-aligned bump allocation in chained blocks, a no-op `free`, and every block freed when the arena is dropped; no `reset()`, since resetting under live objects is the undecided safety question. An object made in an allocator holds a reference to it plus the allocator's `free` (two pointers in the object, written into the struct only for classes some line places -- D177: no other class changes, no registry, no current-allocator state), and gives its memory back through them when its count reaches zero, so an arena outlives everything made in it. A function written by name (`say_hello`, `greeter.greet`) instantiates the element template once per function and owner class with the owner as a hidden last parameter, so it allocates nothing and calls directly -- tree-shaken like every template, and free at run time; a function held in a variable is called through its `Spite.Function`. `map(f)`/`filter(f)` fuse in chains with the member templates (D105), the fused function taking each owner. | 2026-09-25 | (implements D162; the readings below proposed by Claude, unconfirmed) **An arithmetic operator whose right operand is wider than its left is a compile error, and so is a constant expression that overflows `Int`.** Wider is more bits (8: `Tiny`, `Byte`; 16: `Short`, `UnsignedShort`; 32: `Int`, `UnsignedInt`, `Float`; 64: `Long`, `UnsignedLong`, `Double`), or a `Float`/`Double` right side under a whole-number left; signedness alone is not wider. An integer literal (or a negated one) on the right that fits the left type does not count, so `small + 1` on a `Byte` stays legal. **A comparison does not count**: it is not arithmetic, and it keeps casting the right side toward the left exactly as before (so `age > -0.5` on an `Int` is still `age > 0`, open question 3); the C for comparisons is unchanged. The message names the rule and the fix -- `'count * total' is a multiplication in Int, since arithmetic takes the left side's type, and the right side is a Long, which would be cut to fit: write the Long first ('total * count'), or store the right side in an Int first if it fits one`; for `-`, `/` and `%` the fix is `store the left side in a Long first ('var wide: Long = count')`. A constant is a tree of `Int` literals under `+ - * / %`; the first operation whose value leaves `Int` is reported once, with its value: `'(65536 - 120) * 65536' is 4287102976, which does not fit in an Int, the type its arithmetic is done in, so it would wrap: write the number itself, 4287102976, which is a Long`. Both checks happen at compile time and emit nothing. Rewritten: 17 sites, all in `library/` (`daylight_change`, `duration` three times through a new `_nanoseconds_per_unit`, `local_date` three, `long` two, `unsigned_long` two, `number_text`, `tzif_reader` four) plus `examples/hello`; outputs and allocations are unchanged. `diagnostics/wider_right_operand`, `docs/values_and_types.md`. |

What runs them is an event loop over unfinished frames that waits on one operating-system event with the nearest timer as its timeout; helper threads signal it when a blocking call returns (chosen over IOCP/epoll/kqueue because ordinary files are always "ready" to epoll and kqueue and the Windows console cannot be read through IOCP). Code outside a `Concurrent` waits by running that loop; with nothing else alive it is the plain blocking call. A program without `Concurrent` carries none of it. Supersedes the "stackful fibers" row of 2026-09-24. `conformance/stage6/state_machines`, `concurrent_sockets`; `docs/concurrency.md`. |

Strict libraries fail the same way (Go's `UnsupportedValueError`, Gson, `System.Text.Json`); writing `null` as JavaScript and `serde_json` do was rejected as a silent failure, and a `String?` result from `write()` as noise on every caller for what is almost always a bug. | 2026-09-26 | **D199** (decided by Mortaro): **`crash` is for what the compiler can prove away, for what leaves the program unable to work, and for a developer's mistake; everything else is a result the caller handles.** "crash should be for things that we can at compile time verify so ai removes the chance of crash or things that would force the program not to work anymore" -- and, confirming D198 after weighing it, "division by zero means the dev did something wrong and he should be aware of it". A condition the program can meet in normal use (a missing file, a user's bad input, an absent record) answers `T?` or an empty value (D24, D26), never a crash; a library `crash` must be one the compiler can show the program how to avoid, or a bug in the program that wrote the value. Weighed with D198: a `Float` that became infinity or not-a-number came from a division by zero or an overflow the program did not guard, so `Json` crashing on it tells the developer about their mistake rather than failing in normal use. |

They are dealt with where they cannot be represented (D198). |
| 2026-09-26 | **D201** (decided by Mortaro): **division by zero follows Go.** A whole-number division or remainder (`/`, `%`) whose divisor is zero crashes, naming the line and the operation, since it is the developer's mistake (D199); a divisor the compiler can see is zero (a constant, or a codegen value folded to zero) is a compile error instead; where D169's proofs already show the divisor is not zero, no check is emitted. | 2026-09-26 | (implements D203; the size, the layout and the readings below proposed by Claude, unconfirmed) **A `String` is a 16-byte value, and text of up to 15 bytes lives inside it.** The value is `_bytes` and `_length`, two machine words, passed and stored by value everywhere a `String` goes. Its last byte says which form it is: text of up to 15 bytes fills the value itself, with `15 - length` in that byte (0 when the text is 15 bytes long, so the byte after the text is always 0, for C and for `code_at` past the end); longer text is one heap block -- a reference count, its capacity and the characters with a 0 after them -- that `_bytes` points into, the length in the rest of `_length`; a written text points at the program's own characters and is never counted (`'constant'`, as before, whatever its length: a literal costs nothing to make, and C reads it where it is). `String?`'s null is a form of its own (a constant with no characters), so a nullable `String` is still sixteen bytes. 15 and not 22: of the 4 711 225 texts the compiler made compiling itself, 67.9% are 15 bytes or fewer and 78.3% are 22 or fewer, and 22 needs a third word in every `String` where 15 fits in the two a long text needs anyway. Short text allocates nothing and long text allocates once instead of twice (object and characters), so pinned counts drop: `text_building` 35 to 31, `singleton_counts` 104 to 69 (200 104 to 200 069 once merged, where it fetches through holder objects), `fused_chain_allocations` 15 to 13. Retain, release, making text from bytes, joining, appending in place (which fills the value first and moves to a block with room to grow once the text passes 15 bytes) and the text handed to C (`argv`, foreign calls, `DynamicLibrary`, `Console`, crash messages) are the generated C that reads the form; everything else in `library/string.spite` is Spite over `_bytes` and `_length`, which the compiler reads through the form (`_bytes` of a short text is the address of the value itself, so it is read only from `this` or a parameter). A `String` that travels as a `type` shape is put in a box like a number, since the value is not an object: one allocation for text made while the program runs, none for a written text, whose box is static. `.memory.section` of a short text made at run time is `'stack'` held in a local and `'heap'` read from an attribute; of a long one, `'heap'`. Tree-shaken like any other generated C, and nothing runs that did not before: reading the form is a shift and a compare. `docs/optimizations.md` ("Short text lives inside the `String`", and its boxing section). |

Every such decision is recorded here as "decided by Claude under D205" with the measurement, so Mortaro can overturn it; anything that adds syntax, weakens a check or costs speed still waits for him. |
| 2026-09-26 | **D206** (decided by Claude under D205, answering `mortaros_missing_decisions.md` item 199): **a `type` value that is a parameter of the running function may hold borrowed `Vector` items for that call.** It is the narrowest form that lets an engine's `update_each(row: Row)` read and write components in place: the row is never stored, returned or captured (D204's rules apply to it as to a local borrow), and the call may not reach anything that resizes a vector the row borrows from (D169's call effects, the `grow:`/`shrink:` facts). No new syntax; the checks are compile-time, so nothing runs; measured on a stress-shaped program at 1.0 ms per tick against 5.9 ms with `List` columns. |
| 2026-09-26 | **D207** (decided by Claude under D205, answering `mortaros_missing_decisions.md` item 192): **a `Parallel` task may keep an attribute holding an object or a list when the compiler proves its instance is the only holder after `Parallel(...)` is made**, and may always keep a `List` of plain values (numbers, `Boolean`, enums, text). Anything else D179 refused stays refused, and a use of the handed-over object after `Parallel(...)` by the code that made it is a compile error naming the task. No new syntax; the proof is compile-time and the task runs exactly as before; it removes the text-joining workarounds SlopEngine had to write (recipe ids as comma-joined text, lists as newline-joined text). |
| 2026-09-26 | **D208** (decided by Mortaro, revising D138): **`Json` becomes a `JsonWriter` and a `JsonReader`, and a `BinaryWriter` and `BinaryReader` join them with the same shape.** "lets also split Json into a JsonWritter and a JsonReader it looks like it makes more sense, but that also allows us to have a BinaryWritter and a BinaryReader that Slop can use for its network and to serialize resources to disk" (spelled `Writer`). Writing takes the object, as D138 decided (`JsonWriter(order)`); reading names the class it makes. The binary pair writes the same attribute walk as compact bytes -- for networking and for resources on disk -- and D198's refusal of infinity and not-a-number is JSON's alone. Tree-shaken when unused. |
| 2026-09-26 | **D209** (decided by Mortaro, relayed by the SlopEngine session): **code that does IO is known to wait at compile time, so an engine can run whole IO tasks between frames without the programmer shuttling requests and results.** "we also need to think of databases, kal project uses mongodb, but the way Zig was dealing with calls having to set a result that runs in another system and back and forth was making me lose track of things. we should have some kind of system that automatically knows it runs io code and can wait for entire tasks between frames without clogging performance". The compiler already knows every function that can reach a wait (D176); it exposes that as a compile-time fact a program can ask, folded like `has_function` (spelling proposed by Claude, unconfirmed: `$system_type.function_waits("update_each")`), so an engine starts such a function as a `Concurrent`, polls `finished` each frame and never blocks, while the game writes straight-line code (`var account = accounts.find_one(filter)`). A program may also choose where `Concurrent`s resume (proposed: `Scheduler().run_ready()` between frames, with joins of `Parallel` handles that do not run the event loop), so IO work never interleaves with a stage. |

| 2026-09-26 | (implements D209's resume control; both spellings and the reading below proposed by Claude, unconfirmed) **`Scheduler().resume_only_when_asked()` makes `Concurrent`s resume only at `Scheduler().run_ready()` and at a join of a `Concurrent`.** `run_ready(): Integer` steps each ready frame once without blocking (and does nothing off the scheduler's thread). After `resume_only_when_asked()`, a wait outside a state machine on the program's thread steps no frame -- with no REPL it is the plain blocking call -- and joining a `Parallel` runs nothing after it. Reading or dropping a `Concurrent` still steps every frame until that one is done, since it may wait on another, so no join waits for a loop that no longer runs; a loop that polls `finished` without calling `run_ready()` never ends, which is documented rather than detected. Constants are answered by the class object, since they belong to no value: `Float.pi()`, `tau()`, `euler_number()` (not `e()`: a name is never one letter), `infinity()`, `not_a_number()`, `largest()`, `smallest()` (the most negative finite value), and `largest()`/`smallest()` on each whole number; on a value, `angle.pi()` is an error naming `Float.pi()`. The answer is the compiling machine's C library's, so a transcendental function folded on one system and run on another may differ by the last bit (`docs/optimizations.md`). The compiler now calls the C library's maths, so building the seed on Linux and macOS takes `-lm` (`check.sh`, `bin/spite`, `benchmarks/run.sh`, the build commands in `README.md` and `docs/`). `benchmarks/maths_stopgaps` also runs SlopEngine's newer `power_of_two` against `powf`: 199 ms against 97 ms with it, 187 against 40 without. |

| 2026-09-27 | **D255** (decided by Claude under D244, implementing it for native faults; the design, the field names and the readings proposed by Claude, unconfirmed) **A native fault is reported, never silent: every program installs a fault handler first thing in `main`, in every build.** An access violation, a stack overflow, an illegal instruction, `SIGBUS` or `SIGFPE` used to end a program with nothing printed (Theseus has one that comes and goes). Windows: `SetUnhandledExceptionFilter` plus `SetThreadStackGuarantee` (16 KB); Linux and macOS: `sigaction` on a 64 KB alternate signal stack per thread; each thread the program starts sets up its own. The handler allocates nothing, writes with `WriteFile`/`write`, and prints `spite.fault<TAB>kind<TAB>path:line<TAB>Class<TAB>function<TAB>address=<TAB>code=/signal=<TAB>at=file+offset<TAB>foreign=<TAB>library=<TAB>from=`, the assert ring as a crash does, then `spite.frame` lines for the Spite functions on the stack (consecutive repeats folded as `repeated=`, at most 16 lines from 256 frames), flushes stdout and ends with the system's own status for the fault. Which function: a table the compiler writes after every other function, from what tree shaking kept (start address, file and class, name and first line; 32 bytes and the name each), looked up through the system's unwind data on 64-bit Windows, which also walks the stack in every build, and as the nearest start below the address on Linux and macOS, which walk frame pointers where a build keeps them (every build without `--optimized`, and inspectable builds, now compiled with `-fno-omit-frame-pointer` there: about 1% on the compiler, so not in production). **The last foreign call** is one store of a pointer to fixed text into a thread-local before each call, **in every build** (D214: 1.33 ns a call with and without it over 300 000 000 calls). About 3.7 KB of code, the only C nothing tree-shakes. `conformance/stage6/native_fault_foreign`, `native_fault_stack`, `native_fault_illegal` (run on Windows; `check.sh` drops the `+0x` offset, which is the C compiler's); [failure.md](failure.md#what-a-native-fault-reports), [foreign_libraries.md](foreign_libraries.md#when-a-foreign-call-faults), [optimizations.md](optimizations.md#the-fault-handler-is-in-every-program). |

| 2026-09-27 | **D257** (decided by Claude under D205 and D244; the argument-side twin of D220 and D230): **a borrowed item may be passed as an ordinary argument, lent for the duration of that call.** The callee reads and writes it in place and may pass it on as an argument itself; it may not store it in an attribute or collection, return it, or capture it -- those are errors in the callee naming the lent parameter -- and D206's no-resize rule holds for the call (call effects). The compiler proves "keeps nothing" per parameter the way escape analysis already does; a callee that keeps the parameter makes the call site an error naming what keeps it. And the error must never suggest `.copy()` for a parameter the callee writes, since the write would land on the copy and vanish -- that advice was itself a silent failure (D244). Still open under D244: text assigned to an enum that names none of its values becomes the first value. `conformance/stage6/text_to_number`, `diagnostics/text_to_number`, [standard_library.md](standard_library.md#string), [values_and_types.md](values_and_types.md#numeric-types--implemented-provisional), [failure.md](failure.md#nothing-fails-silently--the-rule), [for_ai_writers.md](for_ai_writers.md#nothing-null-and-failure). |
| 2026-09-27 | (implements D244; the messages proposed by Claude, unconfirmed) **A statement ends with its line: whatever is left on the line after it is a parse error at the first word left over.** A statement other than `if`, `while` and `switch` already had to end its line (`expected end of statement but found 'attribute'`); the message now says what is wrong: `'attribute' is left over after the end of the statement: a statement ends with its line, so remove it, or put it on a line of its own if it is a statement`. An `if`, `while` or `switch` did not: after the `}` that closes one, the parser read the rest of the line as the next statement, so `while ... { ... } console.print(total)` compiled as two statements and the formatter moved the second onto its own line without a word; it is now `'console' is left over after the '}' that closes this statement: ...`. Reported from SlopEngine, where `return every attribute at its default` (text from D245's message, pasted into `Slot.fetch`) was said to compile in 22 programs; the parser of this branch and of master refuses that line as it stands, in every position tried (a plain function, a generic class, a folded `if $slot_type == Entity`, a loaded package), so how it compiled there is not reproduced. Nothing in the compiler, `library/`, `conformance/`, `examples/`, `benchmarks/`, `tests/` or the docs' programs relied on either. `diagnostics/left_over_after_return`, `diagnostics/left_over_after_block`, [control_flow.md](control_flow.md#control-flow--implemented), [for_ai_writers.md](for_ai_writers.md#habits-from-other-languages-that-spite-rejects). |
| 2026-09-27 | (implements D245; proposed by Claude, unconfirmed) **The guard-`assert` error offers only answers that are code.** For a result that is a class, the message offered `return a value ('if not ready { return ... }')` and described the default as `(every attribute at its default)`, and that text was pasted into SlopEngine's `Slot.fetch` as `return every attribute at its default`. Now the answer it offers is a constructor call only where one compiles as written -- `'if not ready { return Mark() }'` for a class made with no arguments that is not a singleton, outside a generic class -- and for any other class it offers only `make the result 'Badge?', or 'crash ready'`; the default is described as `(an object with every attribute at its default)`. `diagnostics/default_answer` (a `Badge` made with an argument added), [failure.md](failure.md#assert-is-control-flow). |
